individual paraphrase review pending","followUpExplanation":"The cerebellum has traditionally been considered a major part of the motor system, but it is now known to be important in timing sensory processes.","followUpParagraphIndex":20061}],"classroomParagraphs":["Axons from the motor cortex do not go directly to the muscles, but to the brainstem and spinal cord, which send impulses to the muscles.","Although each hemisphere mainly controls the muscles on the contralateral side, both hemispheres contribute to planning movements on both sides.","The cerebral cortex is necessary for complex actions such as talking or writing, but not for coughing, sneezing, gagging, laughing, or crying.","The lack of cerebral control may explain why it is hard to perform those actions voluntarily. The motor cortex is just anterior to the somatosensory cortex, and the two match up nicely.","Within any area of the motor cortex, the organization represents coordinated movements, not just single muscle contractions.","For many years, researchers studied the motor cortex in laboratory animals by stimulating neurons with brief electrical pulses, usually less than 50 milliseconds (ms) in duration, producing brief muscle twitches. Later researchers found different results when they lengthened the pulses to half a second.","The motor cortex orders an outcome and leaves it to the spinal cord and other areas to find the right combination of muscles.","After more intense stimulation at the same locations, people report that they believe they did make the movement—although, in fact, they did not.","Researchers sometimes use fMRI to measure brain responses while people are preparing to make a movement.","Or people see a first signal with partial information about what they will have to do, and after a","In each case, the posterior parietal cortex is active during the delay, evidently preparing the movement. The supplementary motor cortex in the prefrontal cortex is also essential for planning and organizing movements.","After mild electrical stimulation of the supplementary motor cortex, people report an urge or desire to make a movement, and after stronger stimulation, they actually make a movement.","Activity in the supplementary motor cortex begins at least a second before the start of a voluntary movement and builds up gradually.","It receives information about the target to which the body is directing its movement, as well as information about the body’s current position and posture. Both kinds of information are necessary to direct a movement toward a target.","Parts of the prefrontal cortex are important for considering the probable outcomes of movements.","Or you start to swing at a tennis ball in a doubles match when your partner shouts, “let it go,” because it will land out of bounds.","In cases like these, two brain areas are sending competing messages, and the outcome depends on whether the stop message arrives in time to cancel the action message.","Another example—not an important behavior for its own sake, but a convenient one for psychologists to study— is the antisaccade task. A saccade (sa-KAHD) is a voluntary eye movement from one target to another.","People generally agree that it is easier to look at the finger that moved than the finger that didn’t.","Before age 5 to 7 years, most children find it almost impossible to look away from the wiggling finger. That is, the brain prepares to inhibit the unwanted action and substitute a different one.","A discovery in neuroscience that excited many psychologists was mirror neurons, which are active both during a movement and while watching someone else perform the same movement.","Mirror neurons were first reported in the premotor cortex of monkeys and later in other areas and other species, including humans.","The theoretical interest comes from the possibility that we use mirror neurons to understand other people or to identify with them. One speculation was that a lack of mirror neurons might be responsible for autism or schizophrenia, disorders associated with deficient social relationships.","If mirror neurons are going to explain imitation or empathy, the mirror neurons would have to come first.","The only evidence for inborn mirror neurons came from reports that some newborn infants imitate certain adult facial expressions, such as tongue protrusion.","A review of the literature over decades found that six research groups have reported positive findings, whereas 10 groups have not. Mirror neurons are activated not only by seeing an action but also by any reminder of the action, such as hearing an action or reading about it.","Messages from the brain must reach the medulla and spinal cord, which control the muscles.","Paths from the cerebral cortex to the spinal cord are called the corticospinal tracts.","The lateral corticospinal tract extends from the primary motor cortex, nearby areas of the cortex, and the The medial corticospinal tract extends from many areas of the cerebral cortex, as well as from several areas of the midbrain and medulla (Figure 7.11).","Axons of the medial tract go to both sides of the spinal cord about equally, where they control the muscles of the neck, shoulders, and trunk and therefore movements as walking, turning, bending, standing up, and sitting down.","An easy way to remember the functions of the lateral and medial tracts is that the lateral tract controls muscles in the lateral parts of the body, such as hands and feet.","Note that both paths cross in the medulla and that the touch information arrives at brain areas side by side with those areas responsible for motor control. For example, someone with impaired control of the hand muscles might move the shoulders and trunk in a way that repositions the hand.","The term cerebellum is Latin for “little brain.” Decades ago, most texts described the function of the cerebellum as “balance and coordination.” Well, yes, people with cerebellar damage do lose balance and coordination, but that description understates the importance of this structure.","The cerebellum has more neurons than the rest of the brain combined, a huge number of synapses, and many functions.","The cerebellum is essential for any behavior that relates to aim or precise timing of short intervals. Here is a quick way to test the cerebellum: Hold up your hands and ask someone to look at one hand and then quickly look to the other hand.","This move function depends on the cerebellar cortex (the surface of the cerebellum), which sends messages to the cerebellar nuclei (clusters of cell bodies) in the interior of the cerebellum (Figure 7.13).","This hold function depends on the nuclei alone.","Finally, the finger moves to the nose by a slower movement that does not depend on the cerebellum. A police officer testing someone for drunkenness may use the finger-to-nose test or similar tests because the cerebellum is one of the first brain areas that alcohol affects.","The cerebellum processes information through a highly organized circuit. It receives signals from the spinal cord, sensory systems, and cerebral cortex; these pathways converge on cerebellar circuits, whose output helps regulate activity elsewhere in the brain.","Motor-related signals from the cerebral cortex reach the cerebellum through the pons. Sensory and spinal inputs also supply information about the ongoing state of the body.","Traditionally, the cerebellum has been emphasized for its role in movement, but it has reciprocal connections with all parts of the cerebral cortex, enabling it to contribute to almost everything the brain does.","The chapter on learning and memory will discuss the role of the cerebellum in classical conditioning.","In one study, people were told to keep their eyes fixated on a central point.","At various times, they would see the letter E on either the left or right half of the screen, and they were to indicate the direction in which it was oriented (E, Because the circuit diagram of cells in the cerebellum is nearly uniform throughout, researchers and theorists have tried to describe a single function for the cerebellum, but so far without notable success.","Perhaps it is no more reasonable to seek “the” function of the cerebellum than to seek “the” function of the cerebral cortex.","The direct pathway from the striatum inhibits the globus pallidus, which inhibits part of the thalamus.","Neuroscientists long believed that the direct pathway stimulates movements whereas the indirect pathway inhibits them.","However, later evidence found that both pathways are active before a movement and neither is active when the animal is at rest. A popular hypothesis is that the direct pathway enhances the selected movement whereas the indirect pathway inhibits competing movements.","This path inhibits actions quickly, when it is important to change plans suddenly.","In trials when it heard a signal indicating exactly when to move, the basal ganglia exhibited little activity. In another study, people used a computer mouse to draw lines on a screen while researchers used PET scans to examine brain activity.","Activity in the basal ganglia increased when people drew a new line but not when they traced a line already on the screen. Because cells in the primary motor cortex become active before those in the basal ganglia, the basal ganglia must not be responsible for selecting which movement to make.","Many cells in the basal ganglia cells respond strongly to signals indicating that a response will probably lead to reward.","Separate sections of the basal ganglia respond to the recent value associated with a stimulus and the long-term average value associated with it. Cells in the basal ganglia store longterm memories of the reward value of many objects, more than 90 in one study.","Each of us has the feeling, “I consciously decide to do something, and then I do it.” It seems so obvious that we might not even question it, but research casts doubt on this assumption.","On the average, people report that their decision to move occurred about 200 ms before the actual movement. (The decision occurred then.","People report it later.) For example, if you reported that your decision to move occurred at position 25, your decision came 200 ms before the movement that began at position 30. (Remember, the light moves around the circle in 2.56 seconds.) However, your motor cortex produces a kind of activity called a","readiness potential for any voluntary movement, and on average, the readiness potential begins at least 500 ms before the movement. The research has been replicated in other laboratories, so the results are solid.","One study found that some neurons in the supplementary motor cortex begin activating more than a second before the reported time of the decision.","Many have assumed that if people can accurately state the time of a stimulus or a movement, they are equally accurate at stating the time of a conscious decision. Remember, the letters changed only twice a second, so it wasn’t possible to determine the time of decision with great accuracy.","However, it wasn’t necessary, because parts of the frontal and parietal cortices showed activity specific to the left or right hand 7 to 10 seconds before the response.","It is tempting to describe behavior in three steps—first we perceive, then we think, and finally we act.","The cerebellum has traditionally been considered a major part of the motor system, but it is now known to be important in timing sensory processes.","People with basal ganglia damage are slow to start a movement, and they are also slow at making any kind of choice.","Organizing a movement is not something we tack on at the end of our thinking. develops gradually, because the movement is self-initiated, the type that depends on the basal ganglia.","Human infants are at first limited to gross movements of the trunk, arms, and legs.","The important distinction is between input processing in the cerebellar cortex and output from the deep cerebellar nuclei. This arrangement supports the coordination and adjustment of movement.","The cerebellar cortex contains organized circuits including granule cells and Purkinje cells. They transform incoming activity before it influences the output nuclei.","Processing reaches the deep cerebellar nuclei, which send output toward the cerebral cortex through the thalamus and toward other downstream motor centers.","The separation of input, processing, and output provides a framework for understanding how the cerebellum contributes to movement timing, correction, and coordination."],"classroomResearch":{"title":"Evidence in context: The Cerebral Cortex","body":"Later researchers found different results when they lengthened the pulses to half a illustrate how motor disorders arise from different changes in neural systems. Their symptoms extend beyond overt movement and can affect cognition and everyday functioning."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The book examines mechanisms, heredity, prediction, and treatment. Keep the distinction between reducing symptoms and reversing a disease process clear when studying the available interventions."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-3-study-1","text":"Parkinson’s disease"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Parkinson’s disease commonly involves slowed initiation of movement, rigidity, and tremor, although symptom patterns vary. Loss of dopamine-producing neurons that project to basal ganglia circuits disrupts the regulation of movement. Because the affected networks influence more than movement, people may also experience other changes. Understanding the relevant circuit is more informative than saying only that dopamine is 'low': the consequences depend on which pathways receive altered dopamine signaling."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-3-study-2","text":"Treatments and their limits"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"L-Dopa provides a precursor from which the brain can produce dopamine and can reduce important movement symptoms. Other treatments may alter dopaminergic signaling, and selected patients may benefit from approaches such as deep-brain stimulation. Rehabilitation and other non-drug interventions can support function. None of these methods is a simple cure that reverses every aspect of the underlying process. Treatment decisions depend on individual benefits, side effects, and stage of illness."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-3-study-3","text":"Huntington’s disease"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Huntington’s disease is a hereditary condition that affects movement and additional aspects of behavior and cognition. It is associated with a mutation in the huntingtin gene. Because its inheritance pattern carries implications for family members, genetic testing raises questions about predicting disease risk in someone who does not yet show symptoms. Presymptomatic testing requires careful understanding of what a result can and cannot tell a person."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m7-3-study-4","text":"Compare the two disorders"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Both disorders implicate basal ganglia networks, but they differ in characteristic movements, biology, and inheritance. Parkinson’s often emphasizes difficulty initiating and controlling movements; Huntington’s can involve unwanted movements alongside other changes. Avoid the misconception that a movement disorder is 'only a motor problem.' The effects of disrupted circuits can extend into motivation, emotion, and thinking."},{"type":"heading","id":"deep-bp14-m7-3-h01","text":"Parkinson’s Disease"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Parkinson’s Parkinson’s disease is caused by degeneration of dopamine-containing axons from the substantia nigra to the caudate nucleus and putamen.","paragraphIndex":20038,"printedPages":[254]},{"label":"Underlying mechanism","explanation":"The degeneration of dopamine axons results from both genetic predispositions and environmental toxins.","paragraphIndex":20039,"printedPages":[254]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"textbook_module_summary","sourceFactCount":4,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending"}],"classroomParagraphs":["Parkinson’s disease results from the gradual loss of dopamine-releasing axons going from the substantia nigra to the striatum.","genetically defined type of dopamine neuron in a limited portion of the substantia nigra.","Parkinson’s disease is a progressive disease—one that gradually progresses to a worse and worse outcome.","It affects more than 1 percent of people over age 60, with increasing prevalence at higher ages. People with Parkinson’s disease sometimes move normally in response to signals or instructions, such as while following a parade, but their spontaneous, self-initiated movements are slow and weak.","Many, but not all, Parkinson’s patients may experience depression, and many have problems with attention, language, or memory.","Early-onset Parkinson’s disease (usually defined as before age 40 to 50) has high heritability, but the far more common late-onset condition has lower heritability.","Researchers have identified genes that can cause early-onset Parkinson’s disease, and many genetic variants that slightly raise the risk of late-onset Parkinson’s disease.","Having a brother or sister with the condition increases the risk more than does having a parent or child with the condition. The pattern of results indicates that genes contribute to the risk, but factors in the environment might be even more important, especially for late-onset Parkinson’s.","The substance responsible for the symptoms was MPTP, a chemical that the body converts to MPP 1, which accumulates in, and then destroys, neurons that release dopamine 1.","No one supposes that Parkinson’s disease often results from using recreational drugs, but much research suggests increased risk among workers with much exposure to pesticides (e.g., farmers) or metals (welders and miners).","Exposure to harmful chemicals increases the risk especially among people with any of the genes that predispose to Parkinson’s. In contrast, 13.8 percent of the smokers developed the disease, as did only 11.6 percent of the heaviest smokers.","Because Parkinson’s disease results from a dopamine deficiency, a logical goal is to restore the missing dopamine.","A dopamine pill would be ineffective because dopamine does not cross the blood–brain barrier.","Physicians in the 1950s and 1960s reasoned that L-dopa, a precursor to dopamine that does cross the barrier, might be a good treatment. In contrast to all the medicines that were discovered by trial and error, this was the first drug in psychiatry or neurology, and one of the first in all of medicine, to emerge from a plausible theory.","Taken as a daily pill, L-dopa reaches the brain, where neurons convert it to dopamine, partially restoring movement and other behaviors.","They are more effective in some people than in others, probably because of variation in the intestinal bacteria that metabolize L-dopa before it can enter the blood. The side effects are usually tolerable, but they can include nausea, restlessness, sleep problems, low blood pressure, repetitive movements, and sometimes hallucinations and delusions.","If dopamine replacement becomes ineffective or unsatisfactory, physicians sometimes implant electrodes to stimulate the subthalamic nucleus or the globus pallidus.","The stimulation facilitates desired movements and inhibits unintended movements.","One hypothesis is that people with Parkinson’s disease have excessive synchrony of firing in the basal ganglia, and the stimulation breaks up unhealthy rhythms.","The limitation has been that continuous brain stimulation drains the battery attached to the electrodes. Basic research on the basal ganglia revealed that the electrical stimulation stimulates two types of neurons with different effects.","So far, this procedure has been tried only with laboratory mice, but it has much potential for use with humans.","Control animals that suffered the same brain damage without receiving grafts showed little or no recovery.","This is only a partial brain transplant, but still, the Frankensteinian implications are striking. Fetal neurons transplanted into the brain of patients with Parkinson’s sometimes survive for years and make synapses with the patients’ own cells.","However, the operation is expensive and difficult, requiring brain tissue from four to eight fetuses aborted at just the right stage of development.","Huntington’s disease is a severe neurological disorder that affects about 17 per 100,000 Americans of European ancestry, less than half that many Europeans in Europe itself, probably fewer Africans—although the data for Africa are sparse—and very few Asians.","Motor symptoms usually begin with arm jerks and facial twitches that gradually spread to other parts of the body and develop into writhing.","Because the output from the basal ganglia is inhibitory to the thalamus, damage to the basal ganglia leads to increased activity in parts of the thalamus, producing the involuntary jerky movements.","People with Huntington’s disease also suffer psychological disorders including apathy, depression, sleeplessness, memory impairment, anxiety, hallucinations and delusions, poor judgment, alcoholism, drug abuse, and sexual disorders ranging from complete unresponsiveness to indiscriminate promiscuity. Huntington’s disease can have onset at any age, but most often between the ages of 30 and 50.","Like Parkinson’s disease, Huntington’s is a progressive disease.","Of all the conditions we consider in this text other than color vision deficiency, Huntington’s disease is the only one that can be traced to a single gene.","That gene is an autosomal dominant gene (i.e., not on the X or Y chromosome).","As a rule, a mutant gene that causes the loss of a function is recessive. The fact that the Huntington’s gene is dominant implies that it produces the gain of an undesirable function.","Most people who are at risk choose not to be tested, and about half of those who do get tested do not return to the clinic to learn the results.","As is often the case, people try to avoid threatening or unpleasant information. The critical area of the gene is a sequence of bases C-A-G (cytosine, adenine, guanine), repeated 11 to 24 times in most people.","That repetition produces a string of 11 to 24 glutamines in the resulting protein. People with more repetitions are nearly certain to get the disease, unless they die of other causes earlier.","The mutant form impairs neurons and glia in several ways, including effects on mitochondria and potassium channels.","In the early stages of the disease, it increases neurotransmitter release, sometimes causing overstimulation of the target cells. Identifying the abnormal huntingtin protein and its cellular functions has enabled investigators to search for drugs that might be helpful.","Parkinson’s disease and Huntington’s disease result from brain deterioration that includes the basal ganglia.","Parkinson’s disease is characterized by impaired initiation of activity, slow and inaccurate movements, tremor, rigidity, depression, and cognitive deficits. Parkinson’s disease is caused by degeneration of dopamine-containing axons from the substantia nigra to the caudate nucleus and putamen.","The degeneration of dopamine axons results from both genetic predispositions and environmental toxins."],"classroomCoverage":{"status":"source_structured_full_book","level":"comprehensive_book_derived","topicHeadings":7,"bullets":27,"newWords":1092,"allTopicsWithExplanations":true,"sourceScope":"verified_pdf_topic_facts_crosschecked_with_uploaded_markdown"}},{"id":"bp14-m8-1","chapterId":"bp14-ch08","moduleId":"bp14-m8-1","moduleNumber":"8.1","title":"Rhythms of Waking and Sleeping","kind":"detailed_source_grounded_notes","printedStart":258,"printedEnd":268,"pdfStart":276,"pdfEnd":286,"content":[{"type":"paragraph","text":"Sleep and waking follow endogenous circadian rhythms that are coordinated with environmental cues. The suprachiasmatic nucleus and responses to light help align the body’s clock with the outside world."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Jet lag, night work, and individual differences in daily preference illustrate what happens when biological timing and daily demands do not match. Track how clock mechanisms, melatonin, and behavior interact."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m8-1-study-1","text":"Biological rhythms are built in"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Many bodily processes vary across roughly daily cycles, including alertness, hormone secretion, and temperature. Circadian rhythms are endogenous, meaning they can continue even when ordinary external time cues are absent. Nonetheless, light and daily routines help reset them to the environment. The distinction between an internal oscillator and an outside cue is essential: light usually synchronizes a system rather than creating each individual rhythm from nothing."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m8-1-study-2","text":"The suprachiasmatic nucleus"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A small hypothalamic region called the suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) acts as a central circadian timing system in mammals. Input related to environmental light helps adjust its timing. Molecular feedback mechanisms inside cells support recurring rhythms, and the SCN influences other neural and hormonal processes. Melatonin is associated with the night phase and can convey timing information, but it is not a substitute for understanding the whole clock system."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m8-1-study-3","text":"Jet lag, shift work, and chronotype"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Travel across time zones may abruptly change the clock time outside while internal rhythms remain closer to the old schedule. The resulting mismatch is jet lag. Night work can similarly put sleep and activity out of step with some biological timing processes. Individuals differ in whether they naturally prefer earlier or later schedules, often described as morningness and eveningness. These variations make sleep timing a biological as well as a practical issue."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m8-1-study-4","text":"What to remember about resetting"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Light exposure at different times can shift the circadian clock in different directions, so timing matters as much as amount. It is difficult to adjust to a sleep schedule much different from 24 hours.","paragraphIndex":20055,"bullets":[{"label":"Core idea","explanation":"It is easier for most people to follow a cycle slightly longer than 24 hours (as when traveling west) than to follow a cycle shorter than 24 hours (as when traveling east). Unlike an electric appliance that stays on until someone turns it off, the brain periodically turns itself on and off.","paragraphIndex":20057,"printedPages":[266]},{"label":"Important detail","explanation":"Our biological mechanisms prepare us to wake at certain times and sleep at other times, even if we would prefer other schedules.","paragraphIndex":20058,"printedPages":[266]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"textbook_module_summary","sourceFactCount":5,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending","followUpExplanation":"Even when people set their waking and sleeping times by the clock, the timing of sunrise influences their circadian rhythm.","followUpParagraphIndex":20056}],"classroomParagraphs":["An animal that produced its behavior entirely in response to current stimuli would be at a serious disadvantage.","For example, migratory birds start flying toward their winter homes before their summer territory becomes too cold.","A small bird that waited for the first frost would be in serious trouble.","Similarly, squirrels begin storing nuts and putting on extra layers of fat in preparation for winter long before food becomes scarce. In the tropics, the temperature and amount of daylight are nearly the same throughout the year.","Even if they are kept in a cage with no clues to the season, they become restless in the spring, and if they are released, they fly north.","Even in this unchanging environment, the animal generates a consistent rhythm of activity and sleep.","Although they try to sleep on this 18-hour schedule, their bodies generate rhythms of alertness and body chemistry that continue to average a bit more than 24 hours, and their alertness suffers. We have circadian rhythms in our hunger, thirst, liver activity, insulin secretion, intestinal activity, DNA repair, and much else.","Most showed increases in positive mood (happiness) from waking until late afternoon, and then a slight decline until bedtime.","In addition to light, other zeitgebers include exercise, arousal, meals, and the temperature of the environment.","Social stimuli—that is, the effects of other people—are ineffective as zeitgebers, unless they induce exercise or other vigorous activity.","Although these additional zeitgebers modify the effects of light, they have only weak effects.","People who are working in Antarctica during the constant darkness of an Antarctic winter maintain an approximately 24-hour rhythm, but different people generate slightly different rhythms, until they find it more and more difficult to work together.","When we try to set our wake–sleep cycles by the clock, sunlight still has its influence.","Particularly impressive evidence for the importance of sunlight comes from a study in Germany.","Researchers asked adults for their preferred times of awakening and going to sleep and determined for each person the midpoint of those values. However, those who are not as sensitive to these secondary zeitgebers produce circadian rhythms that are a little longer than 24 hours.","When their cycles are in phase with the clock, all is well, but when they drift out of phase, they may experience insomnia at night and sleepiness during the day.","A disruption of circadian rhythms after crossing time zones is known as jet lag.","Travelers complain of sleepiness during the day, sleeplessness at night, and sometimes depression and impaired concentration.","Most people find it easier to adjust to crossing time zones going west than east. Going west, we stay awake later at night and then awaken late the next morning, already partly adjusted to the new schedule.","Stress elevates blood levels of the adrenal hormone cortisol, and prolonged elevation of cortisol damages neurons in the hippocampus, an area important for memory.","One study examined flight attendants who had spent the previous 5 years making flights across seven or more time zones—such as Chicago to Italy— usually having breaks of fewer than 6 days between trips. These results suggest a danger from repeated adjustments of the circadian rhythm, although the problem here could be air travel itself.","People who work on a night shift, such as midnight to 8 a.m., sleep during the day.","On average, night-shift workers have more accidents than day-shift workers.","It is difficult to adjust the circadian rhythm to work at night, because most buildings use artificial lighting in the range of 150 to 180 lux, which is only moderately effective in resetting the rhythm. People adjust better if they sleep in a very dark room during the day and work under very bright lights at night, comparable to the noonday sun.","Short-wavelength (bluish) light resets the circadian rhythm more strongly than long-wavelength light does.","Some people (“morning people” or “larks”) awaken early, reach their peak of productivity early, and become less alert later.","Others (“evening people” or “owls”) warm up slowly, both literally and figuratively, reaching their peak in the late afternoon or evening.","shift workers, morning people are most impaired when working the night shift and evening people are most impaired when working the morning shift. The tendency to stay up later during adolescence occurs in every culture that researchers have studied throughout the world.","From a functional standpoint, we can only speculate as to why staying up late and waking up late might be more advantageous for adolescents than for children or adults.","In low-tech societies without electric lights, people go to sleep about three hours after sunset, seldom awaken during the night, and wake up at sunrise.","Those who are strongly evening types tend to get lower than average test scores, especially in their morning classes. In both adolescence and adulthood, morning people report being happier than evening people, on average, at least partly because their biological rhythms are more in tune with their school or work schedule.","Curt Richter first demonstrated that the brain generates its own rhythms—a biological clock—and he found that the biological clock is insensitive to most forms of interference.","Blind or deaf animals generate circadian rhythms, although they slowly drift out of phase with the external world.","The circadian rhythm remains surprisingly steady despite food or water deprivation, X-rays, tranquilizers, alcohol, anesthesia, lack of oxygen, most kinds of brain damage, or the removal of endocrine organs. or more of induced hibernation often fails to reset the biological clock.","Evidently, the biological clock is a hardy, robust mechanism.","Although cells throughout the body generate circadian rhythms, the main driver of rhythms for sleep and body temperature is the suprachiasmatic (soo-pruh-kie-as-MAT-ik) nucleus, or SCN, a part of the hypothalamus.","It gets its name from its location just above (“supra”) the optic chiasm (Figure 8.7).","If SCN neurons are disconnected from the rest of the brain or removed from the body and maintained in tissue culture, they continue to produce a circadian rhythm of action potentials. A single SCN cell can maintain a circadian rhythm, but not very accurately.","A mutation in one gene causes hamsters’ SCN to produce a 20-hour instead of 24-hour rhythm.","Researchers surgically removed the SCN from adult hamsters and transplanted SCN tissue from hamster fetuses into the adults. When they transplanted SCN tissue from fetuses with a 20-hour rhythm, the recipients produced a 20-hour rhythm.","Mice with genetic defects that destroy nearly all their rods and cones nevertheless respond to light by resetting their biological clocks.","Nevertheless, light resets their circadian rhythms, enabling them to be awake only at night.","The explanation is that the retinohypothalamic path to the SCN comes from certain retinal ganglion cells that have their own photopigment, melanopsin.","These special ganglion cells receive some input from rods and cones, but even without that input, they respond directly to light. Because they respond slowly when light comes on and decline slowly when the light ceases, they respond to the overall average amount of light, not to instantaneous changes in light.","Because television, video games, computers, and eReaders emit mostly short-wavelength light, late-evening exposure to them phase-delays the circadian rhythm, makes it difficult to fall asleep, and impairs alertness the next morning.","Research on production of the circadian rhythm began when studies on the fruit fly Drosophila found genes that produce a circadian rhythm.","Because the feedback cycle takes about 24 hours, a fly can generate a circadian rhythm even in an unchanging environment.","However, in addition to the automatic feedback, light activates a chemical that breaks down the TIM protein about as fast as it forms during the day (as represented in the figure), keeping the fly awake.","The reason is that analyzing the mechanism in flies told researchers what to look for in humans and other mammals. People with certain PER mutations have a circadian rhythm shorter than 24 hours, as if they were moving about a time zone east every day.","Other genetic mutations can cause the reverse condition, a circadian rhythm longer than 24 hours, marked by difficulty waking up on time in the morning.","The suprachiasmatic nucleus regulates waking and sleeping by controlling activity levels in other brain areas, including the pineal gland (PIN-ee-al; Figure 8.7), an endocrine gland located just posterior to the thalamus.","Melatonin is a widespread chemical, found in nearly all animals—sponges are the only known exception—as well as in plants and bacteria.","People who have pineal gland tumors sometimes stay awake for days at a time. In addition to regulating sleep and wakefulness, melatonin also helps control the onset of puberty and adjustments to changes of season, such as hibernation.","Taking a melatonin pill in the evening has little effect on sleepiness because the pineal gland produces melatonin at that time anyway.","Taking melatonin earlier can hasten the onset of sleepiness and shift the circadian rhythm. Melatonin pills are sometimes helpful when people travel across time zones and need to sleep at an unaccustomed time.","Animals, including humans, have circadian rhythms— internally generated rhythms of activity and sleep lasting about 24 hours. It is difficult to adjust to a sleep schedule much different from 24 hours.","Even when people set their waking and sleeping times by the clock, the timing of sunrise influences their circadian rhythm.","It is easier for most people to follow a cycle slightly longer than 24 hours (as when traveling west) than to follow a cycle shorter than 24 hours (as when traveling east). Unlike an electric appliance that stays on until someone turns it off, the brain periodically turns itself on and off.","Our biological mechanisms prepare us to wake at certain times and sleep at other times, even if we would prefer other schedules."],"classroomCoverage":{"status":"source_structured_full_book","level":"comprehensive_book_derived","topicHeadings":11,"bullets":37,"newWords":1615,"allTopicsWithExplanations":true,"sourceScope":"verified_pdf_topic_facts_crosschecked_with_uploaded_markdown"}},{"id":"bp14-m8-2","chapterId":"bp14-ch08","moduleId":"bp14-m8-2","moduleNumber":"8.2","title":"Stages of Sleep and Brain Mechanisms","kind":"detailed_source_grounded_notes","printedStart":269,"printedEnd":280,"pdfStart":287,"pdfEnd":298,"content":[{"type":"paragraph","text":"Sleep passes through different stages, including REM sleep, while brain systems regulate wakefulness and arousal.
